    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.8, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what finally made me agree to come in is that I’ve just sort of stopped caring about the things that usually keep me steady. I’m 51, I’ve always been pretty good at keeping routines, staying busy, having things outside work that kind of reset me.\n\nBut lately, even the stuff I used to look forward to feels like one more thing on a task list. Sailing races, for example—I used to really get into the preparation, checking the gear, thinking through the weather, all of that. Now I keep finding reasons not to go. Same with my Saturday shift at the historical society, which used to feel meaningful to me. And I’ve got projects sitting half-finished in the woodshop that I would normally enjoy tinkering with, but I walk in there and it just feels like a chore, so I put it off again.\n\nIt’s not one dramatic thing, exactly. It’s more that the things that helped me feel like myself don’t seem to pull me in anymore, and that’s been bothering me enough that… well, here I am.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you feel when you’re not engaging in those activities? Do you feel like you’ve lost interest or pleasure in them altogether, or is it more about not having the energy or motivation to do them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’ve lost the pull toward them, if that makes sense. It’s not just that I’m tired and can’t get off the couch. I mean, sometimes I can make myself do plenty of necessary things, especially if someone is counting on me. But with the things that used to balance me out, there’s just this… flatness.\n\nLike with the sailing races, I used to enjoy all the lead-up—the little details, checking lines, thinking through what the wind might do, talking with the other guys. Now I’ll look at the calendar and instead of feeling that little spark, I start thinking, “Do I really need to go?” and then I find some excuse to put it off.\n\nThe historical society is similar. That Saturday shift used to feel worthwhile, like I was connected to something outside of work. Now it feels like another obligation I have to drag myself through. And the woodshop—same thing. I’ve got projects sitting there that I know I would’ve cared about before, but I go in, look at them, and it’s like they’re somebody else’s unfinished projects. I just close the door and leave it for another day.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s not only motivation. It’s more like the interest or pleasure has kind of gone dim. I still know, logically, that these things matter to me, or used to matter to me, but I don’t feel it the same way right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in all, or almost all, activities most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes. I don’t know if I’d say absolutely every activity, every single minute of the day, but it’s been close enough that it worries me. The things that normally keep me balanced just don’t have the same pull anymore.\n\nSailing races used to be something I’d look forward to, even the preparation part, and now I keep putting them off like they’re a chore. My Saturday shift at the historical society feels the same way — it used to feel worthwhile, and now I sort of dread having it on the calendar. Even going into the woodshop, which used to be my way of settling myself, I’ll look at the projects sitting there and just… not care enough to start.\n\nSo over the past couple weeks, yeah, it’s been there most of the day on most days. It’s not that I can’t function at all, but the enjoyment is missing from the things that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “depressed” most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’ve definitely felt off, and kind of dulled out, like I was saying with the sailing and the historical society and the woodshop. But if you’re asking specifically whether I’ve been feeling sad or down all day, day after day… I don’t know that I can clearly say yes to that.\n\nIt’s more like things feel flat or distant. I’ll go through the day and notice I’m not looking forward to much, but I’m not always sitting there feeling miserable exactly. There are moments where I feel low, sure, especially when I realize I’m avoiding things I normally care about. But it doesn’t feel like a constant heavy sadness every day, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least not in a way I can confidently describe as being depressed most of the day nearly every day. More numb or disconnected than actively down.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, even if it wasn’t every single day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. There have been days where I’ve felt low for a good part of the day, yes, but I’d hesitate to say “most of the day” in a definite way. It’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m sad,” and then that stays steady until bedtime.\n\nIt’s more that the day has this gray cast to it, and if something reminds me of how disconnected I’ve been from the things I usually care about, then I can feel pretty down for a while. But then I might get pulled into something practical, or have a normal conversation, and I’m not necessarily feeling sad in that moment. So it kind of comes and goes.\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been some days in the last two weeks where I felt noticeably down for long stretches. I just don’t know if I’d confidently call it most of the day. It’s more uneven than that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days where you felt down or sad for the majority of the day, even if it wasn’t every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can pin it down that precisely. “Several days” maybe, but “the majority of the day” is the part I get stuck on.\n\nThere have definitely been days in the last couple weeks where I’ve felt sad or low for a long stretch, not just five minutes here and there. But I don’t know that I was tracking it closely enough to say, yes, that was most of the day. It tends to come in waves for me — I’ll be going through my routine, then I notice how flat everything feels, and that can pull me down for a while. Then later I might be distracted by work or some errand and I’m not exactly sad in that moment.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say there have been some days like that, but I’m not confident saying it was several days where sadness took up the majority of the day. It’s been more uneven than that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your energy level or felt unusually fatigued, most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I mean, I do get tired like anyone does, especially depending on how the day goes, but I haven’t really noticed this big drop in energy where I’m dragging most of the day nearly every day.\n\nIf anything, I’d have to think pretty hard about it, because it hasn’t stood out to me as the main issue. I can still get through what I need to do, at least energy-wise. There are times I feel kind of worn out or slowed down, but not in a way where I’d say, “Yes, I’m unusually fatigued most of the day, almost every day.” That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nSo… I guess my answer would be no, not clearly. I haven’t been tracking my energy, but I haven’t noticed a consistent fatigue like that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything significant there. My appetite kind of varies in the normal way, I guess — some days I’m more hungry, some days I don’t think much about food until later — but nothing that made me think, “Oh, something’s really changed.”\n\nAnd with weight, I haven’t been checking it closely every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number. But my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone comment that I look like I’ve lost or gained weight. So I’d say no, not anything significant that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly or that you’ve been feeling restless or unable to sit still, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I wouldn’t say my thoughts have felt slowed down nearly every day. Sometimes I have to reread something or I’ll lose my train of thought for a second, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as my mind moving slowly, if that makes sense. It’s more like normal distraction or being preoccupied, not like everything is delayed.\n\nAnd as far as restlessness, I do move around some — I mean, I’ll shift in my chair, get up to do something, pace a little if I’m thinking through a work problem — but I haven’t really had that feeling of being unable to sit still nearly every day. Not in a way that stood out or made me think something was wrong.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not really. I haven’t noticed either of those as a consistent thing over the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt worthless or experienced excessive guilt nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to answer with a simple no. I don’t know if I’d use the word “worthless,” exactly. I don’t walk around thinking I’m useless as a person, not in that direct a way.\n\nBut guilt, yes. It’s sort of this low-grade thing that follows me around. Like this constant dread in the background, and this feeling that I should’ve anticipated more, or caught one more risk before it became a problem. Even when people tell me the right controls were in place, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still find myself going back over it in my head like, “Yeah, but what if I missed something? What if there was one more thing I should’ve seen?”\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s not dramatic every minute of the day, but it’s there a lot. Nearly every day, probably, in some form. More guilt than feeling worthless. It kind of sits with me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or found yourself wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel worn down or just kind of tired of dealing with everything in my own head, but I haven’t been thinking that I want to die, or wishing I were dead.\n\nDeath as a subject might cross my mind in that ordinary way, like if something comes up in the news or someone mentions it, but not in the sense of me wanting that for myself. I haven’t been sitting around imagining it or feeling like I’d be better off gone. That’s not where I’m at.\n\nSo, no — over the past couple weeks, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not nearly every day. I mean, I have moments where I’m distracted or I’ll stare at an email longer than I should because I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a noticeable problem thinking clearly.\n\nWith decisions, especially work decisions, I can still make them. Sometimes I’ll double-check myself more than I used to, or I’ll want to make sure I’m not missing something, but that feels more like being cautious than being unable to decide. And concentration… it comes and goes a bit, like anyone’s does, especially if there’s a lot on my mind, but I haven’t noticed it as this daily, consistent thing where I can’t focus or can’t function.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no — not in the way you’re asking, not nearly every day over the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself having trouble thinking clearly or focusing on tasks, more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Right, I think this is close to what you just asked, and my answer is still mostly no. I haven’t really noticed it being more than usual over the past two weeks.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where I’ll reread something or take a little longer to settle into a task, especially if it’s something tedious or there are a lot of moving parts. But that’s not completely new for me, and it hasn’t felt like, “Wow, I can’t think straight,” or like I’m suddenly unable to focus.\n\nAt work, I can still follow what’s going on, keep track of priorities, make decisions, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll be more deliberate than maybe I used to be, like checking something twice, but I don’t know that I’d call that trouble thinking clearly. It feels more like being careful.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had more trouble focusing than usual. Not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling indecisive or having trouble organizing your thoughts or making decisions, most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — not most of the day, nearly every day. I know we’ve been circling around this a little, and I’m trying to be accurate, because there are definitely moments where I can get a bit stuck in my head about a decision, especially if there are a lot of details to balance. But that’s also kind of the nature of my work, you know? I’m used to sorting through moving parts and timelines and what depends on what.\n\nOver the past two weeks, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there unable to organize my thoughts or make decisions for most of the day. My appetite kind of varies in the normal way, I guess — some days I’m more hungry, some days I don’t think much about food until later — but nothing that made me think, “Oh, something’s really changed.”\n\nAnd with weight, I haven’t been checking it closely every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number. But my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone comment that I look like I’ve lost or gained weight. So I’d say no, not anything significant that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly or that you’ve been feeling restless or unable to sit still, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I wouldn’t say my thoughts have felt slowed down nearly every day. Sometimes I have to reread something or I’ll lose my train of thought for a second, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as my mind moving slowly, if that makes sense. It’s more like normal distraction or being preoccupied, not like everything is delayed.\n\nAnd as far as restlessness, I do move around some — I mean, I’ll shift in my chair, get up to do something, pace a little if I’m thinking through a work problem — but I haven’t really had that feeling of being unable to sit still nearly every day. Not in a way that stood out or made me think something was wrong.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not really. I haven’t noticed either of those as a consistent thing over the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt worthless or experienced excessive guilt nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to answer with a simple no. I don’t know if I’d use the word “worthless,” exactly. I don’t walk around thinking I’m useless as a person, not in that direct a way.\n\nBut guilt, yes. It’s sort of this low-grade thing that follows me around. Like this constant dread in the background, and this feeling that I should’ve anticipated more, or caught one more risk before it became a problem. What if there was one more thing I should’ve seen?”\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s not dramatic every minute of the day, but it’s there a lot. Nearly every day, probably, in some form. It kind of sits with me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or found yourself wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel worn down or just kind of tired of dealing with everything in my own head, but I haven’t been thinking that I want to die, or wishing I were dead.\n\nDeath as a subject might cross my mind in that ordinary way, like if something comes up in the news or someone mentions it, but not in the sense of me wanting that for myself. I haven’t been sitting around imagining it or feeling like I’d be better off gone. Sometimes I’ll double-check myself more than I used to, or I’ll want to make sure I’m not missing something, but that feels more like being cautious than being unable to decide. And concentration… it comes and goes a bit, like anyone’s does, especially if there’s a lot on my mind, but I haven’t noticed it as this daily, consistent thing where I can’t focus or can’t function.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no — not in the way you’re asking, not nearly every day over the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself having trouble thinking clearly or focusing on tasks, more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Right, I think this is close to what you just asked, and my answer is still mostly no. Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call it sadness in the crying sense, exactly, but there have been days where it’s just this heavy, empty feeling that stays with me for a lot of the day.

It’s more like this low-grade dread that follows me around. Like there’s something I missed, or something I should have caught sooner, even when I try to talk myself through it logically. People keep telling me the controls were in place, that I did what I was supposed to do, but I keep coming back to, “Yeah, but what if there was one more risk I should’ve seen?” And then that turns into guilt, and it sort of sits in the background no matter what I’m doing.

So yes, not every single day maybe, but over the past two weeks there have definitely been days where my mood is clearly down for most of the day. It’s persistent enough that I notice it, and it’s hard to shake.

It’s not that I can’t function at all, but the enjoyment is missing from the things that used to matter to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, over the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “depressed” most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’ve definitely felt off, and kind of dulled out, like I was saying with the sailing and the historical society and the woodshop. But if you’re asking specifically whether I’ve been feeling sad or down all day, day after day… I don’t know that I can clearly say yes to that.\n\nIt’s more like things feel flat or distant. I’ll go through the day and notice I’m not looking forward to much, but I’m not always sitting there feeling miserable exactly. There are moments where I feel low, sure, especially when I realize I’m avoiding things I normally care about. But it doesn’t feel like a constant heavy sadness every day, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least not in a way I can confidently describe as being depressed most of the day nearly every day. More numb or disconnected than actively down.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or sad for most of the day, even if it wasn’t every single day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. There have been days where I’ve felt low for a good part of the day, yes, but I’d hesitate to say “most of the day” in a definite way. It’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m sad,” and then that stays steady until bedtime.\n\nIt’s more that the day has this gray cast to it, and if something reminds me of how disconnected I’ve been from the things I usually care about, then I can feel pretty down for a while. But then I might get pulled into something practical, or have a normal conversation, and I’m not necessarily feeling sad in that moment. So it kind of comes and goes.\n\nI guess what I can say is there have been some days in the last two weeks where I felt noticeably down for long stretches. I just don’t know if I’d confidently call it most of the day. It’s more uneven than that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had several days where you felt down or sad for the majority of the day, even if it wasn’t every day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can pin it down that precisely. “Several days” maybe, but “the majority of the day” is the part I get stuck on.\n\nThere have definitely been days in the last couple weeks where I’ve felt sad or low for a long stretch, not just five minutes here and there. But I don’t know that I was tracking it closely enough to say, yes, that was most of the day. It tends to come in waves for me — I’ll be going through my routine, then I notice how flat everything feels, and that can pull me down for a while. Then later I might be distracted by work or some errand and I’m not exactly sad in that moment.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say there have been some days like that, but I’m not confident saying it was several days where sadness took up the majority of the day. It’s been more uneven than that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed a decrease in your energy level or felt unusually fatigued, most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I mean, I do get tired like anyone does, especially depending on how the day goes, but I haven’t really noticed this big drop in energy where I’m dragging most of the day nearly every day.\n\nIf anything, I’d have to think pretty hard about it, because it hasn’t stood out to me as the main issue. I can still get through what I need to do, at least energy-wise. There are times I feel kind of worn out or slowed down, but not in a way where I’d say, “Yes, I’m unusually fatigued most of the day, almost every day.” That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nSo… I guess my answer would be no, not clearly. I haven’t been tracking my energy, but I haven’t noticed a consistent fatigue like that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything significant there. My appetite kind of varies in the normal way, I guess — some days I’m more hungry, some days I don’t think much about food until later — but nothing that made me think, “Oh, something’s really changed.”\n\nAnd with weight, I haven’t been checking it closely every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number. But my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I haven’t had anyone comment that I look like I’ve lost or gained weight. So I’d say no, not anything significant that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed that your thoughts have been moving more slowly or that you’ve been feeling restless or unable to sit still, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I wouldn’t say my thoughts have felt slowed down nearly every day. Sometimes I have to reread something or I’ll lose my train of thought for a second, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as my mind moving slowly, if that makes sense. It’s more like normal distraction or being preoccupied, not like everything is delayed.\n\nAnd as far as restlessness, I do move around some — I mean, I’ll shift in my chair, get up to do something, pace a little if I’m thinking through a work problem — but I haven’t really had that feeling of being unable to sit still nearly every day. Not in a way that stood out or made me think something was wrong.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not really. I haven’t noticed either of those as a consistent thing over the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt worthless or experienced excessive guilt nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to answer with a simple no. I don’t know if I’d use the word “worthless,” exactly. I don’t walk around thinking I’m useless as a person, not in that direct a way.\n\nBut guilt, yes. It’s sort of this low-grade thing that follows me around. Like this constant dread in the background, and this feeling that I should’ve anticipated more, or caught one more risk before it became a problem. Even when people tell me the right controls were in place, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still find myself going back over it in my head like, “Yeah, but what if I missed something? What if there was one more thing I should’ve seen?”\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s not dramatic every minute of the day, but it’s there a lot. Nearly every day, probably, in some form. More guilt than feeling worthless. It kind of sits with me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or found yourself wishing you were dead?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I feel worn down or just kind of tired of dealing with everything in my own head, but I haven’t been thinking that I want to die, or wishing I were dead.\n\nDeath as a subject might cross my mind in that ordinary way, like if something comes up in the news or someone mentions it, but not in the sense of me wanting that for myself. I haven’t been sitting around imagining it or feeling like I’d be better off gone. That’s not where I’m at.\n\nSo, no — over the past couple weeks, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not nearly every day. I mean, I have moments where I’m distracted or I’ll stare at an email longer than I should because I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a noticeable problem thinking clearly.\n\nWith decisions, especially work decisions, I can still make them. Sometimes I’ll double-check myself more than I used to, or I’ll want to make sure I’m not missing something, but that feels more like being cautious than being unable to decide. And concentration… it comes and goes a bit, like anyone’s does, especially if there’s a lot on my mind, but I haven’t noticed it as this daily, consistent thing where I can’t focus or can’t function.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no — not in the way you’re asking, not nearly every day over the past two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself having trouble thinking clearly or focusing on tasks, more often than usual?\n\nPatient: Right, I think this is close to what you just asked, and my answer is still mostly no. I haven’t really noticed it being more than usual over the past two weeks.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where I’ll reread something or take a little longer to settle into a task, especially if it’s something tedious or there are a lot of moving parts. But that’s not completely new for me, and it hasn’t felt like, “Wow, I can’t think straight,” or like I’m suddenly unable to focus.\n\nAt work, I can still follow what’s going on, keep track of priorities, make decisions, that kind of thing. Sometimes I’ll be more deliberate than maybe I used to be, like checking something twice, but I don’t know that I’d call that trouble thinking clearly. It feels more like being careful.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had more trouble focusing than usual. Not in a way that really stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you found yourself feeling indecisive or having trouble organizing your thoughts or making decisions, most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — not most of the day, nearly every day. I know we’ve been circling around this a little, and I’m trying to be accurate, because there are definitely moments where I can get a bit stuck in my head about a decision, especially if there are a lot of details to balance. But that’s also kind of the nature of my work, you know? I’m used to sorting through moving parts and timelines and what depends on what.\n\nOver the past two weeks, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there unable to organize my thoughts or make decisions for most of the day. I can still put things in order, make a list, figure out priorities, respond to things. Sometimes I might take longer than I’d like, or I’ll go back over something to make sure I’m not overlooking a piece of it, but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are scattered all day or like I’m frozen and can’t choose.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed that nearly every day in that kind of persistent way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt that you’ve completely lost interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, such as your sailing races, the historical society, or your woodshop projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. That’s honestly a big part of why I finally agreed to come in.\n\nThe sailing races used to be one of the things that kept me level. It feels like too much effort for something that used to energize me.\n\nSame with my Saturday shift at the historical society. I used to enjoy talking with people, getting into little details about old records or local history. Now I catch myself thinking of it like a chore I’m trying to get out of. And the woodshop — that one bothers me, because tinkering out there used to be how I’d clear my head. I’ve got projects sitting there half-started, tools exactly where I left them, and I keep putting it off.\n\nSo, yes, I’ve definitely lost interest in those things. They don’t feel like rewards right now. They feel like tasks I’m avoiding.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where your mood was clearly depressed—like a persistent sadness or emptiness—for most of the day, even if it wasn’t every single day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Yes… on most days, I think that’s fair to say. I know I already said it’s not always “sad” in the obvious way, but there’s this emptiness or heaviness that’s there for a lot of the day.

It’s more like this low-grade dread and guilt that follows me around. So yes, most days over the past couple of weeks, there’s been a clearly depressed kind of mood for much of the day. It may not look dramatic from the outside, but inside it’s been pretty constant.
